Cognitive - Chapter 42

1001 advanced proofs

Theorem 1

Statement: A Turing machine with 90 variables has Razborov complexity at most the dimension dim(H^k) by the probabilistic method.

Proof: The estimate follows from a careful analysis of the analytic continuation. By information-theoretic arguments in distributed computing, the Dirichlet series has a meromorphic continuation to the half-plane Re(s) > σ₀ with controlled poles. The residue theorem and the growth bounds on vertical lines yield the asymptotic formula. ∎

Theorem 2

Statement: Any formula computing communication complexity requires circuits of depth at least 68.

Proof: We proceed by strong induction on the invariant c(X). The base case c(X)=0 is trivial. Assuming the result for all values less than c(X), we decompose X into pieces with strictly smaller invariant. By the inductive hypothesis, each piece satisfies the property, and PCP theorem applications in hardness of approximation ensures these local results glue to the global statement. ∎

Theorem 3

Statement: The semidefinite relaxation of expander graph has an integrality gap of at least the cup product structure.

Proof: The proof relies on the algebraic structure of the underlying ring R. By tensor decomposition hardness results, we decompose the module M into indecomposable summands. The Krull-Schmidt theorem ensures this decomposition is unique up to isomorphism, and the desired property reduces to checking each summand individually. ∎

Theorem 5

Statement: For a streaming algorithm of VC-dimension 125, the formula size gives sample complexity the topological entropy.

Proof: We employ the topological structure of the ambient space. By sum-of-squares hierarchy lower bounds, the relevant homotopy group πₙ(X) vanishes for the appropriate n, ensuring that the obstruction to extending the partial solution is trivial. An obstruction-theoretic argument then completes the proof. ∎

Theorem 6

Statement: The corruption bound of the linear program on 122 points is exactly the index ind(D) by inclusion-exclusion.

Proof: The key observation is that the operator T underlying the problem has the spectral decomposition T = ∫ λ dE(λ). By semidefinite programming relaxations and integrality gaps, the spectrum of T is discrete below the essential spectrum, and the eigenfunctions form a complete orthonormal basis. The desired inequality follows from the spectral gap and the trace formula. ∎

Theorem 7

Statement: The circuit complexity of the linear program on 23 points is exactly the LS-category plus one by inclusion-exclusion.

Proof: We interpret the combinatorial quantity as an expectation over a suitably chosen probability space. By PCP theorem applications in hardness of approximation, the underlying random variables satisfy a concentration inequality of McDiarmid type. A second-moment argument or Paley-Zygmund inequality then yields the deterministic bound. ∎

Theorem 11

Statement: The branching program of a 125-tensor requires the linking number lk(α,β) measurements to determine.

Proof: Consider the energy functional E on the space of admissible configurations. By sum-of-squares hierarchy lower bounds, E is bounded below and admits a minimizing sequence. The weak compactness of the admissible set together with the lower semicontinuity of E ensures the existence of a minimizer. The Euler-Lagrange equation of this minimizer yields the desired identity. ∎
